I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count integers from 1 to N (inclusive) that have exactly K non-zero digits in base 10.

Key observations:
- N can be very large (up to 10^100), so we can't iterate through all numbers
- K is small (≤ 3), which suggests we can use digit DP (dynamic programming)
- We need to count numbers with exactly K non-zero digits

## Approach: Digit Dynamic Programming (Digit DP)

We'll use a digit DP approach where the state will track:
- Current position in the digit string
- Whether we're still matching the prefix of N (tight constraint)
- Count of non-zero digits so far
- Whether we've started placing non-zero digits (to handle leading zeros)

The state will be: (position, tight, non_zero_count, started)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Convert N to a string to work with digits
2. Use DP with memoization to count valid numbers
3. For each position, try all possible digits (0-9)
4. Keep track of non-zero digits and tight constraint
5. Only count numbers that have exactly K non-zero digits

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    N = input().strip()
    K = int(input().strip())
    
    n = len(N)
    
    # Memoization dictionary
    from functools import lru_cache
    
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def dp(pos, tight, non_zero_count, started):
        # pos: current position
        # tight: whether we're still bounded by N
        # non_zero_count: count of non-zero digits so far
        # started: whether we've placed a non-zero digit (to handle leading zeros)
        
        # Base case: we've processed all digits
        if pos == n:
            # We need exactly K non-zero digits, and we must have at least one digit (started)
            return 1 if non_zero_count == K and started else 0
        
        result = 0
        # Determine the upper bound for current digit
        limit = int(N[pos]) if tight else 9
        
        for digit in range(limit + 1):
            new_tight = tight and (digit == limit)
            new_started = started or (digit > 0)
            new_non_zero_count = non_zero_count
            
            if digit > 0:
                new_non_zero_count += 1
            
            # We can only place a digit if we haven't exceeded K non-zero digits
            if new_non_zero_count > K:
                continue
                
            # If we haven't started and digit is 0, we continue without starting
            # If we have started, we continue with the count
            result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, new_non_zero_count, new_started)
        
        return result
    
    # We need to count from 1 to N, not from 0 to N
    # So we subtract the count of numbers with exactly K non-zero digits that are 0
    # But 0 has 0 non-zero digits, so it doesn't contribute to K ≥ 1
    
    # However, our DP counts numbers from 0 to N. We need to ensure we don't count 0.
    # Since 0 has 0 non-zero digits, and K ≥ 1, it won't be counted anyway.
    
    # But we need to handle the case where we might count numbers with exactly K non-zero digits that are 0
    # Actually, 0 has 0 non-zero digits, so if K ≥ 1, it won't be counted.
    
    # However, we should ensure our DP doesn't include leading zeros incorrectly.
    # Our "started" flag handles this correctly.
    
    # The DP above counts all numbers from 0 to N with exactly K non-zero digits.
    # Since we're only interested in numbers ≥ 1, and 0 has 0 non-zero digits (not K when K≥1),
    # we don't need to subtract anything.
    
    count = dp(0, True, 0, False)
    
    # Let's verify with examples:
    # For N=100, K=1: should be 19
    # The numbers with exactly 1 non-zero digit: 1, 2, 3, ..., 9, 10, 20, 30, ..., 90, 100
    # That's 9 (for single digits) + 9 (for tens: 10,20,...,90) + 1 (for 100) = 19 ✓
    
    print(count)

solve()
```

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count integers from 1 to N (inclusive) that have exactly K non-zero digits in their decimal representation.

Key observations:
- N can be very large (up to 10^100), so we can't iterate through all numbers
- K is small (1 ≤ K ≤ 3), which suggests we can use digit DP (dynamic programming) or combinatorial counting
- We need to count numbers with exactly K non-zero digits

## Approach

Since N can be extremely large (up to 10^100), we need a digit DP approach. The key insight is that we can use digit DP to count numbers with exactly K non-zero digits that are ≤ N.

Digit DP will track:
1. Position in the number
2. Whether we're still bounded by the original number N
3. Count of non-zero digits so far

## Step-by-step solution

1. Convert N to string to work with its digits
2. Use memoization with DP state (position, tight, non_zero_count)
3. At each position, try placing digits from 0 to (current digit limitation)
4. For each valid digit, update the non-zero count
5. Sum up all valid combinations with exactly K non-zero digits

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    N_str = input().strip()
    K = int(input().strip())
    
    n = len(N_str)
    
    # Memoization dictionary
    from functools import lru_cache
    
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def dp(pos, tight, non_zero_count):
        # pos: current position in the number
        # tight: whether we're still bounded by N
        # non_zero_count: current count of non-zero digits
        
        # Base case: we've processed all positions
        if pos == n:
            # We need exactly K non-zero digits
            return 1 if non_zero_count == K else 0
        
        result = 0
        
        # Determine the upper bound for current digit
        upper_bound = int(N_str[pos]) if tight else 9
        
        for digit in range(upper_bound + 1):
            new_tight = tight and (digit == upper_bound)
            new_non_zero_count = non_zero_count + (1 if digit > 0 else 0)
            
            # Only continue if we don't exceed K non-zero digits
            if new_non_zero_count <= K:
                result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, new_non_zero_count)
        
        return result
    
    # Start DP from position 0, tight=True, non_zero_count=0
    return dp(0, True, 0)

print(solve())
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count integers from 1 to N (inclusive) that have exactly K non-zero digits in their decimal representation.

Key observations:
- N can be extremely large (up to 10^100), so we can't iterate through all numbers
- K is small (1 ≤ K ≤ 3), which suggests we can use digit DP (dynamic programming)
- We need to count numbers with exactly K non-zero digits

## Approach

Since the constraints on K are very small (only 1, 2, or 3), we can use a digit dynamic programming approach.

The digit DP will track:
- Position in the number
- Whether we're still tight (constrained by N)
- Count of non-zero digits so far
- Whether we've started the number (to handle leading zeros)

However, given that K ≤ 3, we can also think about this differently:

For each possible number of digits, we can generate all combinations of digits that have exactly K non-zero digits, and count how many of them are ≤ N.

But the digit DP approach is more systematic.

## Digit DP State

I'll define the DP state as:
- `dp[pos][tight][count][started]` = number of ways to fill digits from position pos onwards, given:
  - `tight`: whether we're still bounded by N
  - `count`: number of non-zero digits used so far
  - `started`: whether we've started the number (to handle leading zeros)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Convert N to a string to work with digits
2. Use memoization to implement digit DP
3. For each position, try all possible digits (0-9) that respect the tight constraint
4. Count valid numbers with exactly K non-zero digits

Let me implement this:

```python
def solve():
    N = input().strip()
    K = int(input().strip())
    
    n = len(N)
    
    from functools import lru_cache
    
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def dp(pos, tight, non_zero_count, started):
        # pos: current position (0 to n)
        # tight: whether we're still bounded by N
        # non_zero_count: number of non-zero digits used so far
        # started: whether we've started the number (non-zero digits have been placed)
        
        # Base case: we've processed all positions
        if pos == n:
            # We need exactly K non-zero digits and the number must be non-empty
            return 1 if non_zero_count == K and started else 0
        
        result = 0
        
        # Determine the upper bound for current digit
        upper = int(N[pos]) if tight else 9
        
        for digit in range(0, upper + 1):
            new_tight = tight and (digit == upper)
            new_started = started or (digit > 0)
            new_non_zero_count = non_zero_count + (1 if digit > 0 else 0)
            
            # If we haven't started and digit is 0, we continue with started=False
            # If we're going to start, we need to ensure we don't exceed K non-zero digits
            if new_non_zero_count > K:
                continue
                
            # If we haven't started and digit is 0, we continue without adding to count
            if not started and digit == 0:
                # We continue without starting, so we keep started=False
                result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, non_zero_count, False)
            else:
                # We're either already started or we just started with a non-zero digit
                result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, new_non_zero_count, True)
        
        return result
    
    # Start DP from position 0, tight=True, count=0, started=False
    # We need to handle the case where all digits are 0, which should be excluded
    # Since we only count numbers from 1 to N, we need at least one non-zero digit
    
    total = dp(0, True, 0, False)
    
    # However, our DP counts numbers with exactly K non-zero digits and non-empty
    # The base case ensures we only count when we have exactly K non-zero digits
    # and we've started (so the number is non-zero)
    
    return total

print(solve())
```

- Numbers with exactly 1 non-zero digit: 1, 2, 3, ..., 9, 10, 20, 30, ..., 90, 100
- 1-9: 9 numbers (each has 1 non-zero digit)
- 10,20,...,90: 9 numbers (each has 1 non-zero digit)

```python
def solve():
    N = input().strip()
    K = int(input().strip())
    
    n = len(N)
    
    from functools import lru_cache
    
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def dp(pos, tight, non_zero_count, started):
        # pos: current position
        # tight: whether we're still bounded by N
        # non_zero_count: number of non-zero digits so far
        # started: whether we've started (non-zero digit placed)
        
        if pos == n:
            # We need exactly K non-zero digits and the number must be non-empty
            return 1 if non_zero_count == K and started else 0
        
        result = 0
        upper = int(N[pos]) if tight else 9
        
        for digit in range(0, upper + 1):
            new_tight = tight and (digit == upper)
            new_started = started or (digit > 0)
            new_non_zero_count = non_zero_count + (1 if digit > 0 else 0)
            
            # Pruning: if we already have more than K non-zero digits, skip
            if new_non_zero_count > K:
                continue
                
            # If we're not started and we place 0, we stay not started
            # If we're not started and we place non-zero, we start
            if not started and digit == 0:
                result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, non_zero_count, False)
            else:
                result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, new_non_zero_count, True)
        
        return result
    
    # Start from position 0, tight=True, no non-zero digits, not started
    return dp(0, True, 0, False)

print(solve())
```

def solve():
    N = input().strip()
    K = int(input().strip())
    
    n = len(N)
    
    from functools import lru_cache
    
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def dp(pos, tight, non_zero_count, started):
        # pos: current position
        # tight: whether we're still bounded by N
        # non_zero_count: number of non-zero digits so far
        # started: whether we've started (non-zero digit placed)
        
        if pos == n:
            # We need exactly K non-zero digits and the number must be non-empty
            return 1 if non_zero_count == K and started else 0
        
        result = 0
        upper = int(N[pos]) if tight else 9
        
        for digit in range(0, upper + 1):
            new_tight = tight and (digit == upper)
            new_started = started or (digit > 0)
            new_non_zero_count = non_zero_count + (1 if digit > 0 else 0)
            
            # Pruning: if we already have more than K non-zero digits, skip
            if new_non_zero_count > K:
                continue

            # If we're not started and we place non-zero, we start

                result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, non_zero_count, False)
            else:
                result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, new_non_zero_count, True)
        
        return result
    
    # Start from position 0, tight=True, no non-zero digits, not started

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count integers between 1 and N (inclusive) that have exactly K non-zero digits when written in base 10.

The challenge is that N can be very large (up to 10^100), so we can't iterate through all numbers. We need a digit DP (Dynamic Programming) approach.

## Key Insights

1. Since K ≤ 3, we only need to track exactly how many non-zero digits we have.
2. We need to count numbers with exactly K non-zero digits that are ≤ N.
3. We can use digit DP with states: (position, tight, non_zero_count, started)

## Digit DP Approach

Let me define the DP state:
- `pos`: current position in the number (from left to right)
- `tight`: whether we are still bounded by N (1 if yes, 0 if no)
- `non_zero_count`: number of non-zero digits so far
- `started`: whether we have started placing non-zero digits (to handle leading zeros properly)

However, since we only care about exactly K non-zero digits, we can simplify.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Convert N to a string to work with its digits
2. Use digit DP to count numbers ≤ N with exactly K non-zero digits
3. Handle the case where we might have fewer digits than N

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    N = input().strip()
    K = int(input().strip())
    
    # Convert N to list of digits
    digits = [int(d) for d in N]
    n = len(digits)
    
    from functools import lru_cache
    
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def dp(pos, tight, non_zero_count, started):
        # pos: current position (0 to n)
        # tight: whether we are still bounded by N
        # non_zero_count: number of non-zero digits so far
        # started: whether we have placed a non-zero digit (to avoid counting leading zeros)
        
        # Base case: we've processed all digits
        if pos == n:
            # We need exactly K non-zero digits and we must have started (non-empty number)
            return 1 if non_zero_count == K and started else 0
        
        result = 0
        
        # Determine the upper bound for current digit
        upper_bound = digits[pos] if tight else 9
        
        for d in range(0, upper_bound + 1):
            new_tight = tight and (d == upper_bound)
            new_started = started or (d > 0)
            new_non_zero_count = non_zero_count
            
            # If d > 0, increment non_zero_count
            if d > 0:
                new_non_zero_count += 1
            
            # If we haven't started and d == 0, we stay in "not started" state
            # But we still need to consider this digit
            
            # If we've already started or d > 0, we're in a valid state
            if new_started:
                # We can only proceed if we don't exceed K non-zero digits
                if new_non_zero_count <= K:
                    result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, new_non_zero_count, new_started)
                # If we exceed K, we skip
            else:
                # We haven't started, so d=0 doesn't add to non-zero count
                result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, non_zero_count, new_started)
        
        return result
    
    # Start DP from position 0, tight=True, non_zero_count=0, started=False
    result = dp(0, True, 0, False)
    
    # We need to ensure we don't count the number 0
    # Since our range is from 1 to N, and we start with non-zero digits,
    # the digit DP naturally handles positive numbers.
    
    return result

print(solve())
```

```python
def solve():
    N = input().strip()
    K = int(input().strip())
    
    # Convert N to list of digits
    digits = [int(d) for d in N]
    n = len(digits)
    
    from functools import lru_cache
    
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def dp(pos, tight, non_zero_count, started):
        # Base case: we've processed all digits
        if pos == n:
            # We need exactly K non-zero digits and we must have started (non-empty number)
            return 1 if non_zero_count == K and started else 0
        
        result = 0
        
        # Determine the upper bound for current digit
        upper_bound = digits[pos] if tight else 9
        
        for d in range(0, upper_bound + 1):
            new_tight = tight and (d == upper_bound)
            new_started = started or (d > 0)
            new_non_zero_count = non_zero_count
            
            # If d > 0, increment non_zero_count
            if d > 0:
                new_non_zero_count += 1
            
            # We can only proceed if we don't exceed K non-zero digits
            if new_non_zero_count <= K:
                result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, new_non_zero_count, new_started)
        
        return result
    
    # Start DP from position 0, tight=True, non_zero_count=0, started=False
    result = dp(0, True, 0, False)
    
    return result
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count integers from 1 to N (inclusive) that have exactly K non-zero digits when written in base 10.

Key observations:
- N can be very large (up to 10^100), so we can't iterate through all numbers
- K is small (1 ≤ K ≤ 3), which means we can use digit DP (Dynamic Programming)
- We need to count numbers with exactly K non-zero digits

## Approach

Since N can be up to 10^100, we need to use digit dynamic programming. The state will track:
- Position in the digit string
- Whether we're still bounded by N (tight constraint)
- Count of non-zero digits so far
- Whether we've started the number (to handle leading zeros)

We'll use a recursive function with memoization to count valid numbers.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Convert N to string** to work with individual digits
2. **Define digit DP state**:
   - position: current digit position we're processing
   - tight: whether we're still bounded by N
   - non_zero_count: number of non-zero digits used so far
   - started: whether we've placed a non-zero digit (to handle leading zeros)

3. **Transition**:
   - For each possible digit (0-9), check if it's valid given the tight constraint
   - Update the non-zero count if digit > 0
   - Update the started flag

4. **Base case**: when we've processed all digits, check if non_zero_count == K

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    N = input().strip()
    K = int(input().strip())
    
    # Convert N to list of digits for easier processing
    digits = [int(d) for d in N]
    n = len(digits)
    
    # Memoization dictionary
    memo = {}
    
    def dp(pos, tight, non_zero_count, started):
        # Base case: we've processed all digits
        if pos == n:
            # We need exactly K non-zero digits and the number should be positive
            return 1 if non_zero_count == K and started else 0
        
        # Check if already computed
        state = (pos, tight, non_zero_count, started)
        if state in memo:
            return memo[state]
        
        # Determine the upper limit for current digit
        limit = digits[pos] if tight else 9
        
        result = 0
        for d in range(0, limit + 1):
            new_tight = tight and (d == limit)
            new_started = started or (d > 0)
            new_non_zero_count = non_zero_count
            
            # If d > 0, increment non-zero count
            if d > 0:
                new_non_zero_count += 1
            
            # Only continue if we're within the required non-zero count
            if new_non_zero_count > K:
                continue
                
            # If we're not started and d == 0, we stay in non-started state
            # But we need to ensure we don't go beyond K non-zero digits
            result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, new_non_zero_count, new_started)
        
        memo[state] = result
        return result
    
    # Start DP from position 0, tight=True, 0 non-zero digits, not started
    answer = dp(0, True, 0, False)
    
    # Special handling: if N is 0 or very small, but K=1, we need to check edge cases
    # Actually, since we start from 1, and our DP handles leading zeros properly,
    # and we require started to be true, it should be fine.
    
    print(answer)

solve()
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count integers from 1 to N that contain exactly K non-zero digits when written in base 10.

Key observations:
- N can be very large (up to 10^100), so we can't iterate through all numbers
- K is small (1 ≤ K ≤ 3), which suggests we can use digit DP (dynamic programming)
- We need to count numbers with exactly K non-zero digits

## Approach

This is a classic digit DP problem. We'll use dynamic programming with state:
- Position in the number
- Whether we're still bounded by N (tight constraint)
- Count of non-zero digits so far
- Whether we've started (to handle leading zeros)

The state will be: `(pos, is_tight, non_zero_count, started)`

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Convert N to string** to process digit by digit
2. **Define DP function** that counts valid numbers from position to end
3. **Handle the tight constraint** - if we're tight, we can't exceed N's digits
4. **Track non-zero digits** and ensure we have exactly K
5. **Account for leading zeros** - numbers with leading zeros don't count toward non-zero digits until we start

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    N = input().strip()
    K = int(input().strip())
    
    n = len(N)
    
    # Memoization dictionary
    memo = {}
    
    def dp(pos, is_tight, non_zero_count, started):
        # Base case: we've processed all digits
        if pos == n:
            # We need exactly K non-zero digits and we must have started (non-empty number)
            return 1 if started and non_zero_count == K else 0
        
        # Memoization key
        key = (pos, is_tight, non_zero_count, started)
        if key in memo:
            return memo[key]
        
        # Determine the upper limit for current digit
        limit = int(N[pos]) if is_tight else 9
        
        result = 0
        
        # Try each possible digit from 0 to limit
        for digit in range(0, limit + 1):
            new_is_tight = is_tight and (digit == limit)
            new_started = started or (digit > 0)
            new_non_zero_count = non_zero_count
            
            # If we're choosing a non-zero digit, increment count
            if digit > 0:
                new_non_zero_count += 1
            
            # Only continue if the count doesn't exceed K (we're counting exactly K)
            # But we can still continue if we have fewer than K, we just need to count valid paths
            # The constraint is checked in the final state, so we allow all paths
            # But we need to ensure we don't exceed K in the final state
            
            # We can continue with this digit
            result += dp(pos + 1, new_is_tight, new_non_zero_count, new_started)
        
        memo[key] = result
        return result
    
    # Start DP from position 0, tight=True, 0 non-zero digits, not started
    answer = dp(0, True, 0, False)
    
    # The above counts numbers from 0 to N, but we need from 1 to N
    # We need to subtract the case of 0 (which has 0 non-zero digits)
    # But our DP ensures started=False initially, so 0 is only counted when all digits are 0
    # Let's modify: actually our DP counts numbers with exactly K non-zero digits in the valid range
    
    # Special case: if K=0, we'd count 0, but K>=1 so we don't have to worry
    # However, our DP might count 0 when no digits are selected, but we track started
    
    # Actually, our started flag ensures that numbers are non-empty
    # And we're counting exactly K non-zero digits, so 0 (which has 0 non-zero digits) is not counted when K>0
    
    return answer

print(solve())
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count integers from 1 to N (inclusive) that have exactly K non-zero digits in their decimal representation.

Key observations:
- N can be very large (up to 10^100), so we can't iterate through all numbers
- K is small (1 ≤ K ≤ 3), which suggests we can use digit DP (dynamic programming) or combinatorial counting
- We need to count numbers with exactly K non-zero digits

## Approach

Since N can be very large (up to 10^100), we need a digit DP approach. The standard digit DP counts numbers with certain properties up to a given number.

We'll use digit DP with the following states:
- Position in the number
- Whether we're still bounded by N (tight constraint)
- Count of non-zero digits so far
- Whether we've started placing non-zero digits (to handle leading zeros)

However, given that K ≤ 3, we can also think about it as:
1. Count numbers with exactly K non-zero digits that are ≤ N

We can use a recursive digit DP approach that builds the number digit by digit, keeping track of:
- Current position
- Tight constraint (whether we're still bounded by N)
- Count of non-zero digits used so far
- Whether we've started (to handle leading zeros)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Convert N to a string to work with its digits
2. Implement digit DP that counts numbers with exactly K non-zero digits
3. Handle the tight constraint properly
4. Return the count

```python
def solve():
    N = input().strip()
    K = int(input().strip())
    
    n = len(N)
    
    # Memoization for digit DP
    from functools import lru_cache
    
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def dp(pos, tight, non_zero_count, started):
        # pos: current position in the digit string
        # tight: whether we are still bounded by N
        # non_zero_count: number of non-zero digits so far
        # started: whether we have started placing non-zero digits (to handle leading zeros)
        
        # Base case: we've processed all digits
        if pos == n:
            # We need exactly K non-zero digits and the number should be valid
            return 1 if non_zero_count == K and started else 0
        
        result = 0
        
        # Determine the upper bound for current digit
        upper_bound = int(N[pos]) if tight else 9
        
        for d in range(0, upper_bound + 1):
            new_tight = tight and (d == upper_bound)
            new_started = started or (d > 0)
            
            # If we haven't started and d is 0, we continue with no non-zero digits
            if not new_started:
                # We're still in leading zeros
                new_non_zero_count = non_zero_count
            else:
                # We've started, so if d > 0, it's a non-zero digit
                new_non_zero_count = non_zero_count + (1 if d > 0 else 0)
            
            # If we exceed K non-zero digits, we can prune
            if new_non_zero_count > K:
                continue
                
            result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, new_non_zero_count, new_started)
        
        return result
    
    # Start DP from position 0, tight=True, non_zero_count=0, started=False
    return dp(0, True, 0, False)

print(solve())
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count integers from 1 to N (inclusive) that have exactly K non-zero digits in their decimal representation.

Key observations:
- N can be very large (up to 10^100), so we can't iterate through all numbers
- K is small (1 ≤ K ≤ 3), which suggests we can use digit DP (dynamic programming) or combinatorial counting
- We need to count numbers with exactly K non-zero digits

## Approach

Since N is extremely large (up to 10^100), we need to use digit dynamic programming (digit DP). The idea is to count valid numbers up to N by processing digits one by one.

For digit DP, we need to track:
1. Position in the number
2. Whether we are still bounded by N (tight constraint)
3. Count of non-zero digits used so far
4. Whether we have started placing non-zero digits (to handle leading zeros)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Digit DP State**: 
   - `pos`: current position in the digit string
   - `tight`: whether we are still bounded by N
   - `non_zero_count`: number of non-zero digits used so far
   - `started`: whether we have started placing non-zero digits (to handle leading zeros)

2. **Transition**: For each position, try all possible digits (0-9) given the constraints.

3. **Base case**: When we reach the end of the number, check if non_zero_count equals K.

4. **Optimization**: Since K ≤ 3, we only need to consider small counts of non-zero digits.

Let me implement the digit DP solution:

```python
def solve():
    N_str = input().strip()
    K = int(input().strip())
    
    n = len(N_str)
    
    # Memoization for DP
    from functools import lru_cache
    
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def dp(pos, tight, non_zero_count, started):
        # pos: current position
        # tight: whether we are bounded by N
        # non_zero_count: number of non-zero digits used
        # started: whether we have placed a non-zero digit (to handle leading zeros)
        
        # Base case: we've processed all digits
        if pos == n:
            return 1 if non_zero_count == K else 0
        
        result = 0
        # Determine the upper bound for current digit
        upper_bound = int(N_str[pos]) if tight else 9
        
        for digit in range(0, upper_bound + 1):
            new_tight = tight and (digit == upper_bound)
            new_started = started or (digit > 0)
            new_non_zero_count = non_zero_count
            
            # If digit is non-zero, increment count
            if digit > 0:
                new_non_zero_count += 1
            
            # If we haven't started and digit is 0, we continue without starting
            # If we haven't started and digit is non-zero, we start placing digits
            # If we've already started, we just continue placing digits
            
            # Check if we haven't started and digit is 0 - this is fine
            # But we need to ensure we don't exceed K non-zero digits
            
            # We need to check if new_non_zero_count exceeds K
            if new_non_zero_count > K:
                continue
                
            result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, new_non_zero_count, new_started)
        
        return result
    
    # Start DP from position 0, with tight=True, non_zero_count=0, started=False
    # We need to handle that we start from 1, not 0
    # But our DP naturally handles this since we start with started=False
    
    # However, we need to exclude 0 if it's counted, but we're counting from 1 to N
    # Our DP from 0 to N would include 0, which has 0 non-zero digits
    # So we need to subtract the case where number is 0
    
    # But 0 is not in our range [1, N], so we need to be careful
    
    # Actually, our DP counts from 0 to N. We need to exclude 0.
    # 0 has 0 non-zero digits, so if K > 0, it won't be counted anyway.
    # If K = 0, we would need to handle it separately, but K ≥ 1.
    
    # Since K ≥ 1, numbers with exactly K non-zero digits won't include 0.
    # So our DP result is already what we want.
    
    return dp(0, True, 0, False)

print(solve())
```

```python
def solve():
    N_str = input().strip()
    K = int(input().strip())
    
    n = len(N_str)
    
    from functools import lru_cache
    
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def dp(pos, tight, non_zero_count, started):
        if pos == n:
            return 1 if non_zero_count == K else 0
        
        result = 0
        upper_bound = int(N_str[pos]) if tight else 9
        
        for digit in range(0, upper_bound + 1):
            new_tight = tight and (digit == upper_bound)
            
            # Update non-zero count only if digit is non-zero and we haven't started
            # Actually, non-zero count should only increment when digit > 0
            new_non_zero_count = non_zero_count
            if digit > 0:
                new_non_zero_count += 1
            
            # If we exceed K non-zero digits, skip
            if new_non_zero_count > K:
                continue
                
            # Update started flag
            new_started = started or (digit > 0)
            
            # If we haven't started and digit is 0, we stay not started
            # If we haven't started and digit > 0, we start
            
            result += dp(pos + 1, new_tight, new_non_zero_count, new_started)
        
        return result
    
    # Start from position 0, tight=True, non_zero_count=0, started=False
    result = dp(0, True, 0, False)
    
    return result
```

1. We need to find the minimum number of edges to add to form a simple cycle of odd length (more than one vertex)

## Key Insights

1. **Existing odd cycles**: If the graph already contains an odd-length cycle, we need 0 edges (t=0, w=1).

2. **Graph bipartiteness**: A graph is bipartite if and only if it doesn't contain any odd cycles. If a graph is bipartite, it has no odd cycles.

3. **Bipartite components**: If the graph is bipartite, we need to add edges to create an odd cycle. In a bipartite graph, vertices can be colored with two colors such that no two adjacent vertices have the same color.

4. **Creating odd cycles**: To create an odd cycle in a bipartite graph, we need to add edges between vertices of the same color class. The minimum number of edges needed is 1 (to create a triangle if possible) or more.

5. **When can we create an odd cycle with 1 edge?**
   - If we have at least 3 vertices in a connected component
   - If we can connect two vertices of the same color class (which happens when they're in the same bipartite component)

6. **How many ways to add 1 edge?**
   - We need to count pairs of vertices in the same color class that are not already connected
   - These pairs will form an odd cycle when added

7. **When do we need more than 1 edge?**
   - If the graph has no odd cycles and no two vertices of the same color class are connected
   - Actually, the minimum is always 1 if there's any component with at least 3 vertices and we can form an odd cycle

The key insight is that we can only form odd cycles of length 3 (smallest odd cycle) with 3 edges, and that requires exactly 3 vertices.

So if we have at least 3 vertices and all connected components have size <= 2, then we need to pick 3 vertices (from different components or same) to form a triangle, and add 3 edges.

But any set of 3 vertices can form a triangle. However, we need to check if these 3 vertices can be connected without creating smaller cycles or violating constraints.

Actually, with 3 vertices and no edges, we can add 3 edges to form a triangle. This forms an odd cycle of length 3.

The number of ways is C(n,3) if we have n vertices and no edges, but only if we can form a cycle using any 3 vertices.

However, if we already have some edges, we can't use those. But in our case, the graph has no odd cycles and we're in the bipartite case.

But if all components have size <= 2, then we need to pick any 3 vertices (from the n vertices), and add the 3 edges of a triangle.

However, we might be able to create an odd cycle using fewer edges if we have a component with size >= 3, but we already ruled that out.

So the algorithm:

1. Find connected components and check for odd cycles (bipartiteness)
2. If any component is not bipartite, return t=0, w=1
3. Otherwise (all components are bipartite):
   - For each component of size >= 3, we can add 1 edge to create odd cycle
   - The number of ways is sum over all such components of C(a,2) + C(b,2) where a,b are sizes of the two color classes
   - t = 1
   - If there is no component of size >= 3, then we need to form a triangle:
     - t = 3
     - w = C(n, 3)  ? But is that correct?

Wait, no. We are adding edges to a graph. We want to form exactly one odd cycle. We can form a triangle on any 3 vertices. But do we need to add exactly the 3 edges? Yes.

And we can only form such a triangle if we pick 3 vertices. The number of ways to choose 3 vertices from n vertices is C(n,3).

But is that valid? Yes, because with 3 vertices, we add 3 edges, and it forms a triangle (odd cycle).

However, we might be able to form an odd cycle with more than 3 vertices, but the problem asks for minimum t.

So the minimum t is 1 if any component has size >= 3, else 3.

And w is:
- If any component has size >= 3: sum over components of C(a,2) + C(b,2) for each component (where a,b are sizes of color classes)
- Otherwise (all components size <= 2): C(n,3)

But let's verify with examples:

Example 1: 4 vertices, edges (1,2),(1,3),(4,2),(4,3)
- Components: one component of 4 vertices
- It's bipartite with classes {1,4} and {2,3}, sizes 2,2
- C(2,2) + C(2,2) = 1 + 1 = 2
- So t=1, w=2 ✓

Example 2: Triangle (3 vertices, 3 edges)
- Has an odd cycle, so t=0, w=1 ✓

Example 3: 3 vertices, 0 edges
- All components size 3, so we need to add 3 edges to form a triangle
- w = C(3,3) = 1 ✓

But wait, in example 3, with 3 isolated vertices, can we form an odd cycle with 1 edge? No, because we would have a path. We need 3 edges.

So the algorithm is:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque
import sys
from math import comb

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
        edges.append((a, b))
    
    if n == 0:
        print("0 1")
        return
    
    # Find connected components and check bipartiteness
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    components = []
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if not visited[i]:
            # BFS to find component and check bipartiteness
            component = []
            color = [-1] * (n + 1)
            queue = deque([i])
            color[i] = 0
            visited[i] = True
            
            while queue:
                node = queue.popleft()
                component.append(node)
                
                for neighbor in graph[node]:
                    if not visited[neighbor]:
                        visited[neighbor] = True
                        color[neighbor] = 1 - color[node]
                        queue.append(neighbor)
                    else:
                        # Check if consistent with bipartite coloring
                        if color[neighbor] == color[node]:
                            # Not bipartite, but we continue as we mark all components
                            pass  # We'll check at end, but this should not happen in a well-formed graph
                            
            components.append(component)
    
    # Check if any component has an odd cycle
    # In a connected component, it is non-bipartite if there's an edge between same color
    # We need to check each edge to see if it violates bipartite property
    
    # Actually, we should just check if any edge connects same color
    # But since we built colors, we can check at the end
    
    # Rebuild colors for all components
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    has_odd_cycle = False
    component_colors = []
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if not visited[i]:
            color = [-1] * (n + 1)
            queue = deque([i])
            color[i] = 0
            visited[i] = True
            component = []
            
            while queue:
                node = queue.popleft()
                component.append(node)
                
                for neighbor in graph[node]:
                    if not visited[neighbor]:
                        visited[neighbor] = True
                        color[neighbor] = 1 - color[node]
                        queue.append(neighbor)
                    else:
                        # Check if edge exists between same color
                        if color[neighbor] == color[node]:
                            has_odd_cycle = True
            
            component_colors.append((component, color))
    
    if has_odd_cycle:
        print("0 1")
        return
    
    # Graph is bipartite, no odd cycles
    # Now find components of size >= 3 and calculate ways
    total_ways = 0
    
    for component, color in component_colors:
        size = len(component)
        # Count vertices in each color class
        color_count = [0, 0]
        for node in component:
            color_count[color[node]] += 1
        
        # If component size >= 3, we can create odd cycle by adding edge between same color
        if size >= 3:
            # Ways to choose two vertices of same color
            ways_in_component = comb(color_count[0], 2) + comb(color_count[1], 2)
            total_ways += ways_in_component
        # If size < 3, we cannot create odd cycle with 1 edge from this component
        # We'll handle components of size < 3 later
    
    # Check if there is any component of size >= 3
    has_component_ge_3 = any(len(comp) >= 3 for comp, _ in component_colors)
    
    if has_component_ge_3:
        print("1", total_ways)
    else:
        # All components have size <= 2
        # We must form a triangle on 3 vertices
        # Number of ways to choose 3 vertices from n vertices: C(n,3)
        total_ways = comb(n, 3)
        print("3", total_ways)

solve()
```

```python
from math import comb

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
```

But in a component of size 2 with no edges, we cannot form a triangle.

So the number of ways to choose any 3 vertices from n vertices is C(n,3).

Therefore, the algorithm is:

- If has odd cycle: t=0, w=1
- Else:
  - For each component that has at least one edge and has at least 3 vertices and has at least one pair of vertices with the same color in the original graph, then we can use that
  - But in any connected component with at least one edge, since it's bipartite, if size>=3, then there are vertices of both colors, and since size>=3, there are at least two vertices of the same color class (because if one class has 1, the other has size>=2, so has at least 2 in the larger class)
  - For a component with at least one edge and size>=3, we can create an odd cycle with one edge between same color vertices
  - Number of such pairs: C(a,2) + C(b,2) for a,b sizes of color classes
  - If there is any such component, t=1, w = sum of those
  - If there is no such component, then we must form a triangle with 3 edges on any 3 vertices, so t=3, w = C(n,3)

But for a component with no edges and size>=3, we cannot create a cycle with one edge (no path), so we must use the triangle method.

For a component with one edge and size>=3, we have a path, so there is a path between vertices, so we can create a cycle with one edge.

Therefore, we only consider components that have at least one edge and size>=3.

For components with no edges, we don't get the one-edge method.

So the algorithm is:

1. has_odd_cycle = false
2. For each component, do BFS to get colors and check for odd cycles
3. If has_odd_cycle, output (0,1)
4. Else:
   - total_ways = 0
   - has_component_with_edge_and_ge_3 = false
   - for each component:
     - if component has at least one edge and size >= 3:
         - compute a, b = counts of each color in component
         - ways += C(a,2) + C(b,2)
         - has_component_with_edge_and_ge_3 = true
   - if has_component_with_edge_and_ge_3:
       - t = 1
       - w = total_ways
   - else:
       - t = 3
       - w = C(n,3)

Let me test with example 3: n=3, m=0
- Components: one component of size 3, has no edges
- has_odd_cycle = false
- no component with at least one edge and size>=3
- so t=3, w=C(3,3)=1 ✓

Example 1: 4 vertices, edges (1,2),(1,3),(4,2),(4,3)
- One component, has edges, size=4>=3
- colors: let's say 1:0, 2:1, 3:0, 4:1 (or 1:0,2:1,3:0,4:0 from 4 connected to 2 (1) and to 3 (0) so 4 should be 1)
- 1:0, 2:1, 3:0, 4:1
- a=2 (0), b=2 (1) -> C(2,2)+C(2,2)=1+1=2
- t=1, w=2 ✓

Example 2: 3 vertices, 3 edges (triangle) -> has odd cycle, so t=0, w=1 ✓

Therefore, the final code is:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque
from math import comb

n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph
graph = defaultdict(list)
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

# If no vertices or one vertex, but n>=3 given
visited = [False] * (n + 1)
has_odd_cycle = False
components = []

# We need to detect for each component if it has at least one edge and size>=3
# Also get the colors

for i in range(1, n + 1):
    if not visited[i]:
        # BFS to find component and assign colors
        color = [-1] * (n + 1)
        queue = deque([i])
        color[i] = 0
        visited[i] = True
        component = []
        
        while queue:
            node = queue.popleft()
            component.append(node)
            
            for neighbor in graph[node]:
                if not visited[neighbor]:
                    visited[neighbor] = True
                    color[neighbor] = 1 - color[node]
                    queue.append(neighbor)
                else:
                    # Check if edge exists between same color
                    if color[neighbor] == color[node]:
                        has_odd_cycle = True
        
        # Also, we need to know the number of edges in this component
        # We can't easily get that from the graph, but we can check if there is at least one edge
        # Actually, we can use: if len(component) >= 3, and the component has at least one edge
        # How to know if it has at least one edge? We can count edges in the component, or we can see that if there is any edge between two nodes in the component
        # But we don't have that stored.
        
        # Instead, for each component, we can note that if it has at least one edge, then at least one edge exists between two nodes in the component
        # We can check at the end or during BFS.
        
        # We can store the component and later check for edges, but we can also note that if we are in a component with at least one edge, then in the BFS, we would have visited neighbors, so we can't easily tell.
        
        # Actually, we can do: during the BFS, we can collect all edges in the component and then later count, but that's inefficient.
        
        # Alternatively, we can note that if the component has size >=3 and in the BFS we did not find any edge between same color, then it's bipartite.
        # But to know if it has at least one edge, we can do: if size>=3 and in the component, there is at least one edge between two nodes.
        # We can store the edge list for each component, or we can note that if a component has size>=3 and is connected, then it has at least one edge only if the graph has an edge.
        
        # Actually, we can determine if the component has at least one edge by checking if there is any edge between any two nodes in the component.
        # But we don't have that stored.
        
        # Since we have the graph, we can do: for each component, we can count the number of edges that have both endpoints in the component, but that might be slow.
        
        # But note: if a component has no edges, then during BFS, we only have the starting node, and we never visit neighbors.
        # In our BFS, if we only have the starting node, then no edges.
        
        # Actually, in our BFS, we only visit neighbors of existing nodes, so if there are no edges, we won't visit any other nodes.
        # So if the component has only one or two nodes, then it has no edges.
        
        # Therefore, for a component with size>=3, it must have at least one edge.
        
        # Is that true? Yes, because to have a component of size>=3, there must be at least one path, hence at least one edge.
        
        # Therefore, any connected component of size>=3 must have at least one edge.
        
        # So in our component, if size>=3, then it has at least one edge.
        
        # Therefore, for each component of size>=3 and no odd cycle, we can use it.
        
        components.append((component, color))

# If we found an odd cycle in any component, output (0,1)
if has_odd_cycle:
    print("0 1")
else:
    total_ways = 0
    has_component_ge_3 = False
    
    for component, color in components:
        size = len(component)
        if size >= 3:
            # Count vertices in each color class
            color_count = [0, 0]
            for node in component:
                c = color[node]
                color_count[c] += 1
            # Number of ways to choose two vertices of the same color
            ways_in_component = comb(color_count[0], 2) + comb(color_count[1], 2)
            total_ways += ways_in_component
            has_component_ge_3 = True
    
    if has_component_ge_3:
        print("1", total_ways)
    else:
        # All components have size < 3, so no component of size>=3
        # We must form a triangle on any 3 vertices
        print("3", comb(n, 3))
```

This should be correct. Let me verify with example 3: n=3, m=0
- Components: one component of size 3
- During BFS, if there are no edges, we will only have the starting node, and then we won't visit others.
- Actually, in the for loop, we have i from 1 to n, and for each i not visited, we do BFS.
- If no edges, then for i=1, we start with 1, and graph[1] is empty, so we only get [1]
- Then i=2, not visited, start with 2, only [2]
- i=3, only [3]
- So we have three components of size 1
- So size>=3? no
- Therefore, has_component_ge_3 = false, so t=3, w=C(3,3)=1 ✓

Example 1: has a component of size 4>=3, and no odd cycle, so yes.

Example 2: has odd cycle, so t=0, w=1.

Therefore, the code is correct.

Final code:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque
from math import comb

n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph
graph = defaultdict(list)
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

visited = [False] * (n + 1)
has_odd_cycle = False
components = []

for i in range(1, n + 1):
    if not visited[i]:
        color = [-1] * (n + 1)
        queue = deque([i])
        color[i] = 0
        visited[i] = True
        component = []
        
        while queue:
            node = queue.popleft()
            component.append(node)
            
            for neighbor in graph[node]:
                if not visited[neighbor]:
                    visited[neighbor] = True
                    color[neighbor] = 1 - color[node]
                    queue.append(neighbor)
                else:
                    if color[neighbor] == color[node]:
                        has_odd_cycle = True
        
        components.append((component, color))

if has_odd_cycle:
    print("0 1")
else:
    total_ways = 0
    has_component_ge_3 = False
    for component, color in components:
        size = len(component)
        if size >= 3:
            color_count = [0, 0]
            for node in component:
                c = color[node]
                color_count[c] += 1
            ways_in_component = comb(color_count[0], 2) + comb(color_count[1], 2)
            total_ways += ways_in_component
            has_component_ge_3 = True
    
    if has_component_ge_3:
        print("1", total_ways)
    else:
        print("3", comb(n, 3))
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

After Vitaly was expelled from the university, he became interested in the graph theory.

Vitaly especially liked the cycles of an odd length in which each vertex occurs at most once.

Vitaly was wondering how to solve the following problem. You are given an undirected graph consisting of n vertices and m edges, not necessarily connected, without parallel edges and loops. You need to find t — the minimum number of edges that must be added to the given graph in order to form a simple cycle of an odd length, consisting of more than one vertex. Moreover, he must find w — the number of ways to add t edges in order to form a cycle of an odd length (consisting of more than one vertex). It is prohibited to add loops or parallel edges.

Two ways to add edges to the graph are considered equal if they have the same sets of added edges.

Since Vitaly does not study at the university, he asked you to help him with this task.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers n and m ($3 \leq n \leq 10^{5}, 0 \leq m \leq \operatorname{min}(\frac{n(n - 1)}{2}, 10^{5})$ — the number of vertices in the graph and the number of edges in the graph.

Next m lines contain the descriptions of the edges of the graph, one edge per line. Each edge is given by a pair of integers a_{i}, b_{i} (1 ≤ a_{i}, b_{i} ≤ n) — the vertices that are connected by the i-th edge. All numbers in the lines are separated by a single space.

It is guaranteed that the given graph doesn't contain any loops and parallel edges. The graph isn't necessarily connected.


-----Output-----

Print in the first line of the output two space-separated integers t and w — the minimum number of edges that should be added to the graph to form a simple cycle of an odd length consisting of more than one vertex where each vertex occurs at most once, and the number of ways to do this.


-----Examples-----

4 2
4 3

Output

3 3
1 2
2 3
3 1

Output
0 1

We need to find the minimum number of edges to add to create an odd-length cycle, and count the number of ways to do this.

- Else (all components are non-bipartite or all bipartite components have only size 1 partitions), we need to add more.

So if no component has a partition of size >=2, then we must create a cycle using more edges.

In this case, the only way is to create a triangle on 3 vertices.

We need to pick 3 vertices that are in the same connected component (to ensure connectivity), and add 3 edges to form a triangle.

But can we do it in a different component? Yes.

The total ways is the number of ways to choose any 3 vertices from the entire graph, but only if they are in the same connected component? No, because we need to add edges, and the component must be connected.

Actually, to form a triangle, we need 3 vertices that are mutually connected or can be connected with the added edges.

But if we add edges between 3 vertices, they become connected.

But we need the vertices to be in the same component initially? Not necessarily.

But if vertices are in different components, we need to connect them first.

The minimum edges to add is 3, and we form a triangle on any 3 vertices.

But in the case of isolated vertices, we can form a triangle on any 3 of them.

Actually, for any set of 3 vertices, we can add the 3 edges to form a triangle.

So w = C(n, 3)

And t = 3.

But is this always optimal? Yes, because we can't create an odd cycle with fewer than 3 edges total.

However, we need to ensure that the 3 vertices are not already forming a cycle, but in the isolated case, they don't.

But what if we have a component with 3 vertices and only 2 edges? We might already have a path.

Actually, we can still add 3 edges to form a triangle.

But the minimum t might be less if there's a bipartite component with partition size >=2.

So algorithm:

1. Check if the graph has any odd cycle (non-bipartite component). If yes, t = 0, w = 1.
2. If not, then:
   - Does any component have a partition with at least 2 vertices?
     - If yes: t = 1, w = sum over all bipartite components of C(size(A),2) + C(size(B),2) for partitions A,B
     - If no: then all components have all vertices isolated or in components of size at most 2 with partitions of size 1 each.
       This means each component has at most 2 vertices.
       So we must form a triangle on 3 vertices, which are not in the same component.
       But to add edges between them, we need to connect them.
       However, if we add edges, we're adding to the graph, and we can add edges to form a triangle on any 3 vertices.
       The minimum edges is 3, and there's only one way: the complete graph on any 3 vertices.
       But wait - we can choose any 3 vertices, so w = C(n,3)
       But is that correct? For example, if we have vertices 1,2,3 and no edges, we can add the 3 edges to form a triangle in exactly one way.

So in all cases:

- If there is any odd cycle in the graph: t=0, w=1
- Else:
  - If there exists a connected component that is bipartite and has at least one partition with size >=2: t=1, w = sum over all bipartite components of C(a_i,2) + C(b_i,2) where a_i, b_i are sizes of partitions in that component
  - Else (all components have at most 2 vertices, and all partitions are size 1): t=3, w = C(n,3)

But in the case where a component has 2 vertices, and it's bipartite, we have one vertex in each partition. So each partition has size 1. So no C(size,2) terms.

For example, two vertices connected by one edge: components of size 2, bipartite, partitions of size 1 each. So w=0.

For three isolated vertices: each component has 1 vertex, so no edge can be added within a partition to create a cycle. So we go to t=3, w=C(3,3)=1.

Yes, matches example 3.

Now, is there a case where we have a bipartite component with size 2 and size 2? For example, a path of 4 vertices: 1-2-3-4.

Partitions: 1,3 in one; 2,4 in other. Each partition has size 2. So we can add edges within each partition: 1-3 and 2-4.

Each adds an edge creating a cycle. So t=1, w = C(2,2) + C(2,2) = 1 + 1 = 2.

But the cycle created by 1-3: uses path 1-2-3 and edge 1-3, forming cycle 1-2-3-1, which is length 3 (odd).

Yes.

So the algorithm:

Steps:
1. Build graph from input.
2. For each connected component, perform BFS/DFS to:
   - Find bipartite status and partition sizes.
   - If during BFS we find a conflict, mark the component as non-bipartite.
3. Check if any component is non-bipartite:
   - If yes: t=0, w=1
4. Else (all components are bipartite):
   - For each component, calculate C(a,2) + C(b,2) where a,b are sizes of partitions
   - Sum these values to get total w
   - If total w > 0, then t=1, w=sum
   - Else (no component has partition of size >=2), then t=3, w = C(n,3)

But what about the case where a component has 3 vertices in a path? Size: partitions might be 1,2. For example, 1-2-3: 1 in A, 2 in B, 3 in A. So A has size 2 (1,3), B has size 1 (2). So C(2,2)=1, C(1,2)=0. So we can add edge 1-3, which creates cycle 1-2-3-1 (length 3).

Yes.

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: n=4, m=4, edges 1-2,1-3,4-2,4-3
- Component: all vertices connected, bipartite
- Partitions: {1,4}, {2,3} → sizes 2,2
- C(2,2) + C(2,2) = 1 + 1 = 2
- t = 1, w = 2 → matches

Example 2: n=3, m=3, edges 1-2,2-3,3-1
- This is a triangle, which is odd cycle → t=0, w=1 → matches

Example 3: n=3, m=0
- 3 isolated vertices, each component has size 1, partitions size 1 each
- So no partition has size >=2 → go to t=3, w=C(3,3)=1 → matches

Perfect.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    if m == 0:
        # Special case: no edges
        # We need to form a triangle, so 3 edges
        # Only one way: all pairs
        print(3, 1)
        return
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
        edges.append((a, b))
    
    # Track components and their bipartite status
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    comp_info = []  # list of (is_bipartite, part_a, part_b) for each component
    
    # BFS for each component
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if not visited[i]:
            # Start BFS for this component
            color = [-1] * (n + 1)
            color[i] = 0
            component = []
            
            queue = deque([i])
            visited[i] = True
            
            while queue:
                u = queue.popleft()
                component.append(u)
                
                for v in graph[u]:
                    if not visited[v]:
                        visited[v] = True
                        color[v] = 1 - color[u]
                        queue.append(v)
                    else:
                        if color[v] == color[u]:
                            # Found odd cycle in this component
                            # This component is not bipartite
                            comp_info.append((False, 0, 0))
                            # We'll handle non-bipartite components later
                            # But we need to mark this component and skip the rest
                            # Actually, we can break out and know there's a non-bipartite component
                            break
            else:
                # No conflict found, this component is bipartite
                # Count sizes of partitions
                part0 = 0
                part1 = 0
                for u in component:
                    if color[u] == 0:
                        part0 += 1
                    else:
                        part1 += 1
                comp_info.append((True, part0, part1))
    
    # Actually, I need to do it properly: if I find a conflict during BFS, mark as non-bipartite
    # Reset visited
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    has_odd_cycle = False
    total_ways_in_bipartite = 0
    
    # Collect all components with bipartite info
    components = []
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if not visited[i]:
            color = [-1] * (n + 1)
            color[i] = 0
            component = []
            queue = deque([i])
            visited[i] = True
            
            while queue:
                u = queue.popleft()
                component.append(u)
                
                for v in graph[u]:
                    if not visited[v]:
                        visited[v] = True
                        color[v] = 1 - color[u]
                        queue.append(v)
                    else:
                        # Check if colors conflict
                        if color[v] == color[u]:
                            has_odd_cycle = True
                            # Skip this component - we found odd cycle
                            # But we still need to process other components
                            break
            else:
                # No conflict found in this component
                part0 = sum(1 for u in component if color[u] == 0)
                part1 = sum(1 for u in component if color[u] == 1)
                # Add to bipartite components info
                components.append((True, part0, part1))
    
    # If we found any odd cycle in any component, answer is 0, 1
    if has_odd_cycle:
        print(0, 1)
        return
    
    # All components are bipartite
    # Calculate total ways to add one edge within a partition
    total_ways = 0
    for is_bipartite, part0, part1 in components:
        if part0 >= 2:
            total_ways += part0 * (part0 - 1) // 2
        if part1 >= 2:
            total_ways += part1 * (part1 - 1) // 2
    
    # If no such edge can be added, then we need 3 edges
    if total_ways == 0:
        # All components have partitions of size at most 1
        # We need to form a triangle on any 3 vertices
        # Number of ways: C(n, 3)
        total_ways = n * (n - 1) * (n - 2) // 6
        print(3, total_ways)
    else:
        print(1, total_ways)
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(m):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
    
    # Track visited and find components with odd cycles
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    has_odd_cycle = False
    bipartite_components = []  # list of (part0, part1) for each bipartite component
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if not visited[i]:
            # BFS for component starting at i
            color = [-1] * (n + 1)
            color[i] = 0
            component = []
            queue = deque([i])
            visited[i] = True
            
            found_odd_cycle = False
            
            while queue and not found_odd_cycle:
                u = queue.popleft()
                component.append(u)
                
                for v in graph[u]:
                    if not visited[v]:
                        visited[v] = True
                        color[v] = 1 - color[u]
                        queue.append(v)
                    else:
                        # v is visited, check if it's same color
                        if color[v] == color[u]:
                            found_odd_cycle = True
                            break
            
            if found_odd_cycle:
                has_odd_cycle = True
            else:
                # Count partition sizes
                part0 = sum(1 for u in component if color[u] == 0)
                part1 = sum(1 for u in component if color[u] == 1)
                bipartite_components.append((part0, part1))
    
    # If any odd cycle exists, answer is 0,1
    if has_odd_cycle:
        print(0, 1)
        return
    
    # All components are bipartite
    # Calculate total ways to add edges within partitions (creating odd cycles)
    total_ways = 0
    for part0, part1 in bipartite_components:
        if part0 >= 2:
            total_ways += part0 * (part0 - 1) // 2
        if part1 >= 2:
            total_ways += part1 * (part1 - 1) // 2
    
    # If no way to create an odd cycle with one edge, we need 3 edges (triangle on any 3 vertices)
    if total_ways == 0:
        # Number of ways to choose 3 vertices out of n
        total_ways = n * (n - 1) * (n - 2) // 6
        print(3, total_ways)
    else:
        print(1, total_ways)
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(m):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
    
    # Track visited and find components with odd cycles
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    has_odd_cycle = False
    bipartite_components = []  # list of (part0, part1) for each bipartite component
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if not visited[i]:
            # BFS for component starting at i
            color = [-1] * (n + 1)
            color[i] = 0
            component = []
            queue = deque([i])
            visited[i] = True
            
            found_odd_cycle = False
            
            while queue and not found_odd_cycle:
                u = queue.popleft()
                component.append(u)
                
                for v in graph[u]:
                    if not visited[v]:
                        visited[v] = True
                        color[v] = 1 - color[u]
                        queue.append(v)
                    else:
                        # v is visited, check if it's same color
                        if color[v] == color[u]:
                            found_odd_cycle = True
                            break
            
            if found_odd_cycle:
                has_odd_cycle = True
            else:

                part0 = sum(1 for u in component if color[u] == 0)
                part1 = sum(1 for u in component if color[u] == 1)
                bipartite_components.append((part0, part1))

        return
```

After Vitaly was expelled from the university, he became interested in the graph theory.

Vitaly especially liked the cycles of an odd length in which each vertex occurs at most once.

Vitaly was wondering how to solve the following problem. You are given an undirected graph consisting of n vertices and m edges, not necessarily connected, without parallel edges and loops. You need to find t — the minimum number of edges that must be added to the given graph in order to form a simple cycle of an odd length, consisting of more than one vertex. Moreover, he must find w — the number of ways to add t edges in order to form a cycle of an odd length (consisting of more than one vertex). It is prohibited to add loops or parallel edges.

Two ways to add edges to the graph are considered equal if they have the same sets of added edges.

Since Vitaly does not study at the university, he asked you to help him with this task.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers n and m ($3 \leq n \leq 10^{5}, 0 \leq m \leq \operatorname{min}(\frac{n(n - 1)}{2}, 10^{5})$ — the number of vertices in the graph and the number of edges in the graph.

Next m lines contain the descriptions of the edges of the graph, one edge per line. Each edge is given by a pair of integers a_{i}, b_{i} (1 ≤ a_{i}, b_{i} ≤ n) — the vertices that are connected by the i-th edge. All numbers in the lines are separated by a single space.

It is guaranteed that the given graph doesn't contain any loops and parallel edges. The graph isn't necessarily connected.


-----Output-----

Print in the first line of the output two space-separated integers t and w — the minimum number of edges that should be added to the graph to form a simple cycle of an odd length consisting of more than one vertex where each vertex occurs at most once, and the number of ways to do this.

0 1

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the minimum number of edges to add to form an odd-length cycle, and count the number of ways to do this.

Key observations:
1. We need to form a cycle of odd length (3, 5, 7, ...) with no repeated vertices
2. We want the minimum number of edges to add
3. We need to count the number of ways to achieve this minimum

## Analysis

### Case 1: Graph already contains an odd-length cycle
If the graph already has an odd-length cycle (like a triangle), then we need 0 edges to add. There's exactly 1 way (add 0 edges).

### Case 2: Graph has no odd-length cycle
This means the graph is bipartite. In a bipartite graph, all cycles are even-length. To create an odd-length cycle, we need to break the bipartite property.

In a bipartite graph, if we have a connected component, we can create an odd cycle by adding an edge between two vertices in the same partition. 

But we need to find the minimum number of edges to add and the number of ways.

### Key Insight
- In a bipartite graph, we can create an odd cycle by adding a single edge between two vertices in the same partition of a connected component.
- However, we might be able to create an odd cycle with fewer edges by considering multiple components.

In fact, in a complete bipartite graph K_{a,b}, any edge between different partitions is already present. Adding a new edge between different partitions creates a new cycle, but the parity depends on the existing structure.

I think I need to reconsider the entire approach.

## Correct Approach

After research and thinking, I recall that:

1. If the graph already contains an odd cycle, answer is (0, 1)
2. If the graph is bipartite, we need to add edges to create an odd cycle.

For a bipartite graph, we can create an odd cycle by adding an edge between two vertices that are in the same connected component and in different partitions.

But adding an edge between different partitions creates a cycle of even length if the original graph was bipartite? No.

Actually, in a bipartite graph, any cycle is even. When we add an edge between two vertices u and v, if u and v are in the same partition, we create an odd cycle. 

Let me verify with the K_{2,2} example:

Components: vertex 1 connected to 2 and 3, vertex 4 connected to 2 and 3. Partitions: {1,3} and {2,4}.

If I add edge between 1 and 3 (same partition): do I get an odd cycle?
- Path 1→2→4→3→1: uses edges 1-2, 2-4, 4-3, 3-1. That's 4 edges, even.
- Path 1→3 (new), 3→4→2→1: 3→4, 4→2, 2→1, 1→3: 4 edges, even.

It seems I get even cycles.

If I add edge between 2 and 4 (same partition): 
- Path 2→1→3→4→2: 2→1, 1→3, 3→4, 4→2: 4 edges, even.

Path: 1→2, 2→4, 4→1. That's 3 edges, which is odd.

Yes! So adding an edge between vertices in different partitions can create an odd cycle.

Actually, in a bipartite graph, there's a path between u and v of even length (using the bipartite structure), and the direct edge creates a cycle of odd length.

Wait no: if u and v are in different partitions, the path between them is even length, and adding a direct edge creates a cycle of odd length? Even + 1 = odd.

Yes! If the original path from u to v has even length, adding a direct edge creates a cycle of odd length.

So in a connected bipartite component, for any two vertices in different partitions, adding an edge between them creates an odd cycle.

For vertices in the same partition, adding an edge between them would create a cycle of even length? Because path between them in bipartite graph has odd length, plus direct edge (1) gives even.

So only adding edges between vertices in different partitions creates odd cycles.

Therefore:

1. If graph has odd cycle → (0, 1)
2. If graph is bipartite:
   - For each connected component, if it has at least 2 vertices, we can add edges between vertices in different partitions.
   - The number of such edges in a component with partitions of size a and b is a * b.
   - But we only need to add one edge to create an odd cycle.
   - So minimum t = 1 (we can always create an odd cycle with one edge)
   - Number of ways w = sum over all components of (a * b), where a and b are sizes of partitions in that component.

But what if a component has only one vertex? It has partitions of size 1 and 0, so a*b = 0. Correct.

What if a component has two vertices? a=1, b=1, so a*b=1. Adding edge between them creates a 2-cycle? That's even.

Oh, problem! Cycle length must be > 1 and be odd.

A 2-cycle is not allowed.

Actually, the cycle length formed by adding edge (u,v) is: length = length of path from u to v + 1.

For it to be odd, length of path from u to v must be even.

In a bipartite graph, the distance between two vertices in different partitions is even, between same partition is odd.

So for u,v in different partitions, path length is even, so new cycle length = even + 1 = odd.

And since we have at least 2 vertices in different partitions, we have a path.

The cycle has length at least 3.

For two vertices in different partitions, if there's a path of length 2 between them, then adding edge creates a cycle of length 3.

In a connected bipartite component with 3 or more vertices, there are pairs in different partitions with path length 2.

For example, in K_{2,2}, any two vertices in different partitions have path length 2 (e.g., 1→2→4 for vertices 1 and 4).

So adding edge between 1 and 4 creates cycle 1-2-4-1 of length 3.

Yes! So it works.
